Appendix A
Formulae

A.1
Laplace transforms and RG Evolution

Here we present the details of the resummation of large logarithms via RG evolution. The earliest papers exploring evolution equations, with the type of momentum space convolutions encountered here, were Refs.
Korchemsky:1993uz
;
Balzereit:1998yf
, and further advances to the approach were made in Ref.
Neubert:2005nt
which presented formulas valid to all orders in perturbation theory. These rely on the equations being multiplicative in Laplace or Fourier space. Solutions to these equations can also be obtained functionally to all orders, both directly in momentum space by formulas combining plus functions
Fleming:2007xt
;
Ligeti:2008ac
, or with derivatives to compactly transform logarithms from Laplace to momentum space
Becher:2006nr
;
Becher:2006mr
.
Ref.
Almeida_2014
provides a review and we follow the notation used there.

We consider a generic
\overline{\rm MS}
renormalized SCET matrix element
{\cal F}(q,\mu)
, with
j_{\cal F}
the mass dimensions of
q
.
When
{\cal F}(q,\mu)
enters factorization theorems in a Laplace convolution over the momentum space variable
q
, its renormalization group equation (RGE) reads

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}{\cal F}(q,\mu)=\int dq^{\prime}\,\gamma_{\cal F}(q-q^{\prime},\mu)\,{\cal F}(q,\mu)\,,

(194)

where
\gamma_{\cal F}
is the anomalous dimension in momentum space. In SCET such functions are ubiquitous and result from the fact that UV renormalization of soft and collinear matrix elements accounts for double poles in the dimensional regulator, that are in turn reminiscent of the double IR poles of the corresponding QCD matrix elements. The presence of double poles in counterterms, and the convolution nature of the factorization theorem, lead to nontrivial running such as the one shown in Eq. (
194
). To solve of Eq. (
194
), we consider the Laplace transform of
{\cal F}
given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\cal F}(x,\mu)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-qx}{\cal F}(q,\mu)\,,

(195)

such that
x
has mass dimensions
-j_{\cal F}
. The formula for inverse Laplace transform reads

\displaystyle{\cal F}(q,\mu)=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\frac{dx}{2\pi i}\,e^{qx}\widetilde{\cal F}(x,\mu)\,,

(196)

where the real number
c
determines a vertical contour in the complex plane that leaves any pole of
\widetilde{\cal F}
to its left.
In position space, the RGE is simply multiplicative:

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\widetilde{\cal F}(x,\mu)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{\cal F}(x,\mu)\,\widetilde{{\cal F}}(x,\mu)\equiv\Big[\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]\,\textrm{ln}(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu^{j_{\cal F}})+\gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]\Big]\,\widetilde{{\cal F}}(x,\mu)\,,

(197)

where the anomalous dimension
9
9

9

Had there been higher powers of the logarithm in Eq. (
197
) the factorization into soft and collinear matrix elements would not have been possible. This has been shown to be true explicitly up to four loops
Becher:2019avh
.

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\cal F}(x,\mu)
consists of a cusp piece
\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]
, responsible for resumming double logarithms, and a non-cusp part
\gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]
. The cusp part,
\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]
, is proportional to the cusp-anomalous dimension such that
\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}]=\kappa_{\cal F}C_{\kappa}\Gamma^{\rm cusp}[\alpha_{s}]
, where
C_{\kappa}=C_{F}
or
C_{A}
.
Equation (
197
) has a formal solution

\displaystyle{\cal F}(q,\mu)=\int\!dq^{\prime}\,U_{\cal F}(q-q^{\prime},\mu,\mu_{0})\,{\cal F}(q^{\prime},\mu_{0})=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\!\frac{dx}{2\pi i}\,e^{qx}\,\widetilde{U}_{\cal F}(x,\mu,\mu_{0})\,\widetilde{\cal F}(x,\mu_{0})\,,

(198)

in terms of the evolution operator

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{\cal F}(x,\mu,\mu_{0})=e^{K_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})}\,(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu_{0}^{j_{\cal{F}}})^{\omega_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})}\,.

(199)

The evolution kernels
K_{\cal F}
and
\omega_{\cal F}
are obtained from integrals of the anomalous dimensions,

\displaystyle K_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=K^{\Gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})+K^{\gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})

(200)

\displaystyle K^{\Gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=j_{\cal{F}}\int_{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}^{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}\frac{d\alpha}{\beta[\alpha]}\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha]\int_{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}^{\alpha}\frac{d\alpha^{\prime}}{\beta[\alpha^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle K^{\gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}^{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}\frac{d\alpha}{\beta[\alpha]}\gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha]\,,

\displaystyle\omega_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}^{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}\frac{d\alpha}{\beta[\alpha]}\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha]\,,

involving the QCD beta function
\beta[\alpha_{s}]
, whose perturbative expansion we give in Eq. (
206
).
We also state the results for functions
F(\mu,\mu_{F})
that enter the factorized cross section as multiplicative factors (and not through convolutions) with
\mu_{F}
being the energy scale specific to
F
. Here the RGE is directly given by

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\mu}F(\mu,\mu_{F})=\gamma_{F}(\mu,\mu_{F})F(\mu,\mu_{F})=\Big[\Gamma_{F}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{\mu_{F}}\Big)+\gamma_{F}(\alpha_{s})\Big]F(\mu,\mu_{0})\,,

(201)

with the solution for evolution from scale
\mu_{0}
to
\mu
being

\displaystyle F(\mu,\mu_{F})

\displaystyle=U_{F}(\mu,\mu_{0};\mu_{F})F(\mu_{0},\mu_{F})\equiv e^{K_{F}(\mu,\mu_{0})}\,\Big(\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{F}}\Big)^{\omega_{F}(\mu,\mu_{F})}F(\mu_{0},\mu_{F})\,,

(202)

where the evolution kernels
K_{F}
and
\omega_{F}
are again given by Eq. (
200
). The kernels in Eq. (
200
) depend on the specific function
\cal{F}
(or
F
) only through the anomalous dimensions: we can thus rewrite them in terms of two universal kernels

\displaystyle K^{\Gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})=j_{\cal{F}}K(\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}],\mu,\mu_{0})\,,\quad K^{\gamma}_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})=\eta(\gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}],\mu,\mu_{0})\,,\quad\omega_{\cal F}(\mu,\mu_{0})=\eta(\Gamma_{\cal F}[\alpha_{s}],\mu,\mu_{0})\,,

(203)

whose explicit NLL expressions are

\displaystyle\eta(\Gamma,\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma_{0}}{2\beta_{0}}\bigg[\textrm{ln}r+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}{4\pi}\bigg(\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{\Gamma_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{0}}\bigg)(r-1)\bigg]\,,

(204)

\displaystyle K(\Gamma,\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma_{0}}{4\beta_{0}^{2}}\Bigg[\frac{4\pi}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}\Big(\textrm{ln}r+\frac{1}{r}-1\Big)+\bigg(\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{\Gamma_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{0}}\bigg)(r-1-\textrm{ln}r)-\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{0}}\textrm{ln}^{2}r\Bigg]\,,

(205)

where
r=\alpha_{s}(\mu)/\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})
. Here the perturbative expansion of a generic anomalous dimension
\Gamma[\alpha_{s}]
and
\beta[\alpha_{s}]
is written as

\displaystyle\Gamma[\alpha_{s}]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Gamma_{n}\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\Big)^{n+1}\,,\qquad\beta[\alpha_{s}]=-2\,\alpha_{s}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\beta_{n}\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\Big)^{n+1}\,

(206)

and the terms relevant for NLL resummation read

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}^{\rm cusp}

\displaystyle=4\,,\qquad\Gamma^{\rm cusp}_{1}=4C_{A}\bigg(\frac{67}{9}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\bigg)-\frac{80n_{f}T_{R}}{9}\,,

(207)

\displaystyle\beta_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{11}{3}C_{A}-\frac{4}{3}T_{R}n_{f}\,,\qquad\beta_{1}=\frac{34}{3}C_{A}^{2}-4T_{R}n_{f}\bigg(C_{F}+\frac{5}{3}C_{A}\bigg)\,.

(208)

A.2
One-loop anomalous dimensions

We state here one-loop results for the anomalous dimensions of the various functions appearing in Eq. (
71
) with the cusp and noncusp pieces defined as in Eqs. (
197
) and (
201
). The RGEs of these functions are given by

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log N^{\kappa}(Q,R,\mu)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{N^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q\tan\frac{R}{2}}\Big)+\gamma_{N^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\,,

(209)

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log{\cal C}^{\kappa}\bigg(\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{Q^{2}R_{g}^{2}},QR_{g},\mu\bigg)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{{\cal C}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q\frac{R_{g}}{2}}\Big)+\gamma_{{\cal C}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\,,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log\tilde{J}_{\kappa}(x,\mu)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{J_{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu^{2}\big)+\gamma_{J_{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\,,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log\tilde{S}^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}(y,\mu)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}y\mu\big)+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]\,,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log\tilde{S}^{\kappa}_{c}(z,\beta,\mu)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}z\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\big)+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c}}(\beta)[\alpha_{s}]\,,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log S_{G}^{\kappa}\Big(Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2},R,\beta,\mu\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\beta}\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{G}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2}}\Big)+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{G}}(\beta)[\alpha_{s}]\,,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\log S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\beta}\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\big)^{1+\beta}}\bigg)\bigg]+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}}(\beta)[\alpha_{s}]\,.

and the inverse Laplace transforms for
J_{\kappa}
,
S_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}
and
S_{c}^{\kappa}
are defined as follows,

\displaystyle J_{\kappa}(m_{J}^{2},\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\frac{dx}{2\pi i}\>e^{x\,m_{J}^{2}}\tilde{J}_{\kappa}(x,\mu)\,,

(210)

\displaystyle S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}\Big(\frac{\ell^{+}}{R_{g}/2},\mu\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\frac{dy}{2\pi i}\>e^{y\,\frac{\ell^{+}}{R_{g}/2}}\>\tilde{S}_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}(y,\mu)\,,

\displaystyle S_{c}^{\kappa}\big(\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\mu\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\>e^{z\,\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}}\>\tilde{S}_{c}^{\kappa}(z,\mu)\,,

such that the mass dimensions of
x
,
y
, and
z
are respectively

\displaystyle-j_{J_{\kappa}}=-2\,,\qquad-j_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}=-1\,,\qquad-j_{S_{c}^{\kappa}}=-(2+\beta)/(1+\beta)\,.

(211)

The cusp pieces in Eq. (
209
) are multiples of the universal cusp anomalous dimension
\Gamma^{\rm cusp}[\alpha_{s}]
and are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{S_{G}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]=-\Gamma_{J_{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]=-\Gamma_{{\cal C}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]=\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]=\Gamma_{S_{c}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]=\Gamma_{S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]=-2C_{\kappa}\,\Gamma^{\rm cusp}[\alpha_{s}]\,,

(212)

and the one-loop noncusp pieces are

\displaystyle\gamma^{N^{q}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{J_{q}}_{0}=-\gamma^{{\cal C}^{q}}_{0}=-6C_{F}\,,\quad\gamma^{N^{g}}_{0}=-\gamma^{J_{g}}_{0}=-\gamma^{{\cal C}^{g}}_{0}=-2\beta_{0}\,,\quad\gamma^{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}_{0}=\gamma^{S_{c}^{\kappa}}_{0}=\gamma^{S_{G}^{\kappa}}_{0}=\gamma^{S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}}_{0}=0\,.

(213)

We can now check the RG consistency relation for the factorized cross section for doubly differential
m_{J}^{2}
and
R_{g}
measurements.
Written in position space, the dijet cross section for jet masses is given in the intermediate regime by Eq. (
4.2
). By differentiating with respect to the renormalization scale, it is immediate to show that the sum of the cusp terms must vanish,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q\tan\frac{R}{2}}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{1+\beta}\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{G}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2}}\Big)+\Gamma_{J_{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu^{2}\big)

(214)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\bigg[\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\frac{2}{R_{g}}\bigg]+\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\frac{QR_{g_{L}}}{2}\mu\Big)=0\,,

and that the noncusp pieces must also add to zero from RG consistency,

\displaystyle\gamma_{N^{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]+\gamma_{J_{\kappa}}[\alpha_{s}]+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{G}}[\alpha_{s}]+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]+\gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}}[\alpha_{s}]=0\,,

(215)

as can be easily verified at one loop from Eq. (
213
). This puts nontrivial constraint on the
\beta
dependence of the global-soft and collinear-soft non cusp anomalous dimensions. As a consequence of the refactorization in Eq. (
50
) in the regime of large groomed jet radius, the relation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{g}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\bigg(\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\frac{2}{R_{g}}\bigg)+\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c_{m}}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\Big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\frac{QR_{g}}{2}\mu\Big)=\Gamma_{S^{\kappa}_{c}}[\alpha_{s}]\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}xQQ_{\rm cut}^{\frac{-1}{1+\beta}}\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\big)

(216)

is also enforced by RG consistency.

Appendix B
One-loop results

In this appendix we collect the one-loop results for the ingredients appearing in the double differential cross section. We start from the functions that enter the factorized expression for the cross section cumulant in
R_{g}
and describe their evolution, then we move on to describing the ingredients of the boundary cross section that enters the definition of
C_{2}^{q}
.

B.1
Results for the cumulant distribution

B.1.1
Global-soft function

The global soft function accounts for the soft radiation which is groomed away, namely lies within the original jet but fails the soft drop test. The one loop correction for
e^{+}e^{-}
collisions requires computing

\displaystyle S_{G}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\mu\bigr)=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{(\mu^{2}e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int\frac{dp^{+}dp^{-}}{(p^{+}p^{-})^{1+\epsilon}}\,\Theta_{\rm sd}\,\overline{\Theta}_{R}\,,

(217)

where
C_{q}=C_{F}
and
C_{g}=C_{A}
, the condition for failing soft drop is

\displaystyle\Theta_{\rm sd}

\displaystyle\equiv\Theta\bigg(z_{\rm cut}\Big(2\sin\frac{\theta_{p}}{2}\Big)^{\beta}-\frac{p^{+}+p^{-}}{Q}\bigg)\,,

(218)

with
\sin^{2}(\theta_{p}/2)=p^{+}/(p^{+}+p^{-})
, and
\overline{\Theta}_{R}
constrains radiation within a jet of radius
R
,

\displaystyle\overline{\Theta}_{R}\equiv\Theta\bigg(\tan\frac{R}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{p^{+}}{p^{-}}}\bigg)\,.

(219)

Solving the integration in Eq. (
217
) we get

\displaystyle S_{G}^{\kappa}\Big(Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2},R,\beta,\mu\Big)=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{1}{(1+\beta)}\log^{2}\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2}}\Big)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\Big(\frac{1}{1+\beta}\Big)

(220)

\displaystyle-\frac{(2+\beta)}{4}\Big(2{\rm Li}_{2}\Big[\sin^{2}\Big(\frac{R}{2}\Big)\Big]+\log^{2}\Big[\cos^{2}\frac{R}{2}\Big]\Big)\bigg]\,.

For
pp
collisions with small jet radius,
R\ll 1
, one retrieves the familiar expression

\displaystyle S_{G}^{\kappa}\bigl(p_{T}z_{\rm cut}^{\prime}R^{1+\beta},\beta,\mu\bigr)=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{1}{1+\beta}

\displaystyle\bigg[\log^{2}\Big(\frac{\mu}{p_{T}z_{\rm cut}^{\prime}R^{1+\beta}}\Big)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\bigg]\,.

(221)

B.1.2
Jet function

The one-loop result for the jet function is well known
Bauer:2003pi
;
Bosch:2004th
and given by

\displaystyle J_{\kappa}(m_{J}^{2},\mu)\equiv\delta(m_{J}^{2})+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\pi}\Bigg[\frac{C_{\kappa}}{\mu^{2}}{\cal L}_{1}\Big(\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\Big)-\frac{\gamma_{0}^{J_{\kappa}}}{8}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}{\cal L}_{0}\Big(\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\Big)-\delta(m_{J}^{2})\Big(C_{\kappa}\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}-c_{1}^{\kappa}\Big)\Bigg]\,.

(222)

The Laplace transform is defined as (see Eq. (
210
))

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\kappa}(x,\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dm_{J}^{2}\>e^{-x\,m_{J}^{2}}J_{\kappa}(m_{J}^{2},\mu)\,,

(223)

such that Eq. (
222
) gives

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\kappa}\bigl(x,\mu\bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv 1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{C_{\kappa}}{2}\log^{2}\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu^{2}\big)+\frac{\gamma_{0}^{J_{\kappa}}}{8}\log\big(e^{\gamma_{E}}x\mu^{2}\big)-C_{\kappa}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}+c_{1}^{\kappa}\bigg]\,,

(224)

where the one-loop non-cusp anomalous dimension for the quark and gluon jet functions are

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{J_{q}}

\displaystyle=6C_{F}\,,\qquad\gamma_{0}^{J_{g}}=2\beta_{0}\,,

(225)

the one-loop constant terms being

\displaystyle c_{1}^{q}=\frac{7}{4}\,,\qquad c_{1}^{g}=\frac{\Gamma^{\rm cusp}_{1}}{8}=\frac{C_{A}}{2}\bigg(\frac{67}{9}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\bigg)-\frac{10n_{f}T_{R}}{9}\,.

(226)

Equivalently, following to the alternative notation in Eq. (
4.2
), we write Eq. (
224
) as

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\kappa}\big[\ell_{J},\alpha_{s}(\mu_{J})\big]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{J})}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{C_{\kappa}}{2}\ell_{J}^{2}+\frac{\gamma_{0}^{J_{\kappa}}}{8}\ell_{J}-C_{\kappa}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}+c_{1}^{\kappa}\bigg]\,.

(227)

B.1.3
Collinear-soft functions at intermediate
R_{g}

The CS
g
function describes emissions that live at the groomed jet boundary and pass soft drop. At one loop, it requires computing

\displaystyle S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Big)=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{(\mu^{2}e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int\frac{dp^{+}dp^{-}}{(p^{+}p^{-})^{1+\epsilon}}\,\overline{\,}\Theta\bigg(\frac{R_{g}}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{p_{+}}{p_{-}}}\bigg)\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}\,,

(228)

with

\displaystyle\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}\,=\,1-\Theta_{\rm sd}\,=\,\Theta\bigg(\frac{p^{-}}{Q}-z_{\rm cut}\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{p^{-}}\Big)^{\beta/2}\bigg)\,,

(229)

where the last equality uses that for collinear-soft radiation
p^{+}\ll p^{-}
. This leads to the
e^{+}e^{-}
equivalent of the one-loop result of
Kang:2019prh
,

\displaystyle S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Big)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{1}{1+\beta}\bigg[\log^{2}\bigg(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}(R_{g}/2)^{(1+\beta)}}\bigg)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\bigg]\,,

(230)

The CS
m
function describes emissions at the groomed jet boundary that set the jet mass (therefore, they automatically pass soft drop in this regime). From the bare one-loop expression

\displaystyle S_{c_{m}}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Big)=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{(\mu^{2}e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int\frac{dp^{+}dp^{-}}{(p^{+}p^{-})^{1+\epsilon}}\,\overline{\,}\Theta\bigg(\frac{R_{g}}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{p^{+}}{p^{-}}}\bigg)\delta\Big(\frac{\ell^{+}}{p^{+}}p^{-}-1\Big)\,,

(231)

we get

\displaystyle S_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\Big(\frac{2\ell^{+}}{R_{g}},\mu\Big)

\displaystyle=\delta\Big(\frac{2\ell^{+}}{R_{g}}\Big)+\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{-2}{\mu}{\cal L}_{1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\mu}\frac{2\ell^{+}}{R_{g}}\bigg)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\delta\Big(\frac{2\ell^{+}}{R_{g}}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(232)

which is the equivalent to the result for the soft function for ungroomed jets of radius
R\rightarrow R_{g}
computed in Ref.
Ellis:2010rwa
(up to a different choice in normalization). Following Eq. (
210
) the Laplace transform of
S_{c_{m}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\bigl(u,\mu\bigr)=\int\frac{d\ell^{+}}{R_{g}/2}e^{-u\frac{\ell^{+}}{R_{g}/2}}S_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{\ell^{+}}{R_{g}/2},\mu\Bigr]\,

\displaystyle(\text{$e^{+}e^{-}$ case})\,,

(233)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\bigl(u,\mu\bigr)=\int\frac{dr^{+}}{R_{g}}e^{-u\frac{r^{+}}{R_{g}}}S_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{r^{+}}{R_{g}},\mu\Bigr]\,

\displaystyle(\text{$pp$ case})\,,

such that at one loop (for either scenario)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\bigl(u,\mu\bigr)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[-\log^{2}\big(ue^{\gamma_{E}}\mu\big)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\bigg]\,.

(234)

In the notation of Eq. (
4.2
), useful in our final expressions for the resummed cross sections, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c_{m}}^{\kappa}\bigl[L_{cs_{m}},\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs_{m}})\bigr]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs_{m}})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[-L_{cs_{m}}^{2}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\bigg]\,.

(235)

B.1.4
Collinear-soft functions at large
R_{g}

In the large
R_{g}
case, the collinear-soft function knows about both the mass measurement and the soft-drop condition. At one loop, we need

\displaystyle S_{c}^{\kappa[1],{\rm bare}}\Big[\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Big]=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{(\mu^{2}e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\!\int\!\!\frac{dp^{+}dp^{-}}{(p^{+}p^{-})^{1+\epsilon}}\,\overline{\,}\Theta\bigg(\frac{R^{2}_{g}}{4}-\frac{p^{+}}{p^{-}}\bigg)\delta\Big(\frac{\ell^{+}p^{-}}{p^{+}}-1\Big)\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}\,,

(236)

yielding the result presented in Eq. (
34
). Given our multiplicative treatment of this function, see Eq. (
78
), we need to separate the terms that contribute to RG evolution from the ones that depend on
R_{g}
. The
R_{g}
independent function coincides with the collinear-soft function for single differential jet mass distribution. Its one-loop result in the
\overline{\rm MS}
scheme is

\displaystyle S_{c}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\ell^{+}\,Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\delta\Bigl(\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\Bigr)

(237)

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{\kappa}\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\pi}\Biggl\{\!\frac{-2(1+\beta)}{(2\!+\!\beta)\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}}\,{\cal L}_{1}\!\biggl[\frac{\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}}{\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}}\biggr]+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\frac{2\!+\!\beta}{1\!+\!\beta}\,\delta\Bigl(\ell^{+}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\Bigr)\!\Biggr\},

where
{\cal L}_{1}(x)=\big[\Theta(x)\frac{\textrm{ln}x}{x}\big]_{+}
is the standard plus function which integrates to zero on
x\in[0,1]
.
Following the convention for Laplace transform in Eq. (
82
) we have at one loop

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c}^{\kappa}\bigl(s,\beta,\mu\bigr)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[-\Big(\frac{1+\beta}{2+\beta}\Big)\log^{2}\big(se^{\gamma_{E}}\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\big)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\frac{\beta(3\beta+4)}{(1+\beta)(2+\beta)}\bigg]\,,

(238)

Likewise, Laplace transforms of other pieces appearing in Eq. (
83
) for NLL
′
resummation in the large
R_{g}
region are also defined with respect to the momentum variable
\ell^{+}
following Eq. (
82
), such that at one-loop we find

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\bigl(s,\beta,\mu\bigr)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)\beta_{0}}{2\pi}\Big(\frac{1+\beta}{2+\beta}\Big)\log\big(se^{\gamma_{E}}\mu^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\big)\,,

(239)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{\kappa}]}\Bigl(z=s\,Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\Big)^{2+\beta},\beta,\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}z^{-b}\,\Gamma[b,z]\Bigg|_{b=0}\,,

where the incomplete Gamma function is defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma[b,z]\equiv\int_{z}^{\infty}dt\>t^{b-1}e^{-t}=z^{b}\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\>t^{b-1}e^{-zt}\,,\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial b}z^{-b}\,\Gamma[b,z]\Bigg|_{b=0}=\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\>\frac{\log(t)}{t}e^{-zt}\,.

(240)

In our alternative Laplace space notation, we have for Eqs. (
238
) and (
239
) respectively

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{c}^{\kappa}\big[L_{cs},\beta,\alpha(\mu_{cs})\big]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[-\Big(\frac{1+\beta}{2+\beta}\Big)L_{cs}^{2}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\frac{\beta(3\beta+4)}{(1+\beta)(2+\beta)}\bigg]\,,

(241)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[L_{cs},\beta,\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})\big]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)\beta_{0}}{2\pi}\Big(\frac{1+\beta}{2+\beta}\Big)L_{cs}\,.

(242)

B.2
Derivation of the large
R_{g}
resummed cross section

In order to derive Eq. (
86
) and obtain an explicit expression for the kernel
{\cal Q^{\kappa}}
defined in Eq. (
4.3
) we first write down the cross section in Laplace space in Eq. (
4.3
) with resummation made explicit using Eqs. (
199
) and (
202
):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!dm_{J}^{2}\,\,e^{-x\,m_{J}^{2}}\frac{d\Sigma(R_{g})}{dm_{J}^{2}}\bigg|_{R_{g}\lesssim\theta_{g}^{\star}}\!\!\!=N^{q}_{\rm evol}\big(Q,Q_{\rm cut},\beta,R,\mu;\mu_{N},\mu_{gs}\big)\>e^{K_{cs}(\mu,\mu_{cs})+K_{J}(\mu,\mu_{J})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\frac{\mu_{J}^{2}}{m_{J}^{2}}\Big)^{\tilde{\omega}_{J}(\mu,\mu_{J})}\bigg(\frac{Q\mu_{cs}}{m_{J}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\mu_{cs}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\bigg)^{\omega_{cs}(\mu,\mu_{cs})}\big(m_{J}^{2}xe^{\gamma_{E}}\big)^{\tilde{\omega}(\mu_{cs},\mu_{J})}\tilde{J}_{q}(x,\mu_{J})\,\tilde{S}_{c}^{q}\Bigl(xQQ_{\rm cut}^{-\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu_{cs}\Bigr)\,

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(1+\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\bigl(xQQ_{\rm cut}^{-\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu_{cs}\bigr)\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{F}]}\Bigl(xQQ_{\rm cut}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\Big)^{2+\beta},\beta,\mu_{cs}\Bigr)\bigg)\,.

(243)

Here we have decomposed the collinear-soft function as in Eqs. (
83
) and (
84
). We recognize in the first two lines of Eq. (
B.2
) the resummed, single differential jet mass cross section, described in distribution space by the differential operator in Eq. (
4.3
). We now write
xm_{J}^{2}=\varrho
such that the functions
\tilde{J}_{q}
and
\tilde{S}_{c}^{q}
depend on their arguments only through the logarithms

\displaystyle L_{J}=\log\Big(\frac{\mu_{J}^{2}}{m_{J}^{2}}\Big)+\log(\varrho)+\gamma_{E}\,,\qquad L_{S_{c}}=\log\bigg(\frac{Q\mu_{cs}}{m_{J}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\mu_{cs}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\bigg)+\log(\varrho)+\gamma_{E}\,,

(244)

and note that the argument of the
R_{g}
-dependent piece
\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{F}]}
in Eq. (
B.2
) reduces to the combination

\displaystyle xQQ_{\rm cut}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\Big)^{2+\beta}=\varrho\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{\theta_{g}^{\star}}\Big)^{2+\beta}\,.

(245)

Abbreviating
\tilde{\omega}(\mu_{cs},\mu_{J})=\Omega
and
(R_{g}/\theta_{g}^{\star})^{2+\beta}=\upsilon
, the inverse Laplace transform of Eq. (
B.2
) in terms of dummy variable
r
conjugate to
\varrho
for
r=1
, yields

\displaystyle\frac{d\Sigma(R_{g})}{dm_{J}^{2}}\bigg|_{R_{g}\lesssim\theta_{g}^{\star}}

\displaystyle=N^{q}_{\rm evol}\big(Q,Q_{\rm cut},\beta,R,\mu;\mu_{N},\mu_{gs}\big)

(246)

\displaystyle\times\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}e^{\gamma_{E}\Omega}\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho}\varrho^{\Omega}\bigg(1+\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}(L_{S_{c}},\beta,\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs}))\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{F}]}(\rho\upsilon,\beta,\mu_{cs})\bigg)\,.

Noting from Eq. (
239
) that
\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}
depends on its argument only through the logarithm defined in Eq. (
244
), written in the notation of Eq. (
242
), allows us to rewrite it as a function of
\partial_{\Omega}
and recognize in the second term of Eq. (
246
) the definition of
{\cal Q}^{q}
from Eq. (
4.3
). This completes the derivation of Eq. (
86
). The same steps carry over to the
pp
case, leading to the expression in Eq. (
97
).

To find an explicit expression for
{\cal Q^{\kappa}}
, valid for both
\kappa=q,g
, we take the inverse Laplace transform of
\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{\kappa}]}
from Eq. (
239
), which gives the integral

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho}\varrho^{\Omega}\big(\varrho\upsilon\big)^{-b}\Gamma\big[b,\varrho\upsilon\big]

\displaystyle=\,\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\>t^{b-1}\>\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho(1-\upsilon t)}\varrho^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\Theta(1-\upsilon)}{\Gamma[-\Omega]}\upsilon^{-b}B[1-\upsilon;-\Omega,b]\,.

(247)

Here we used the incomplete Beta function

\displaystyle B[1-\upsilon,-\Omega,b]\equiv\int_{0}^{1-\upsilon}dx\>x^{-\Omega-1}(1-x)^{b-1}\,.

(248)

Including the constant factors, we get the result

\displaystyle{\cal Q}^{\kappa}\Big[\Omega,\upsilon=\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{\theta_{g}^{\star}}\Big)^{2+\beta},\beta\Big]=\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\bigg[\partial_{\Omega}+\log\bigg(\frac{Q\mu_{cs}}{m_{J}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\mu_{cs}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\bigg),\beta,\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})\bigg]

(249)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{e^{\gamma_{E}\Omega}}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg(-\Theta(1-\upsilon)\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\upsilon^{-b}B\big[1-\upsilon;\,-\Omega,b\big]\bigg|_{b=0}\bigg)\,,

where we can equivalently write

\displaystyle\partial_{b}\>\upsilon^{-b}B[1-\upsilon;-\Omega,b]\Big|_{b=0}=\int_{0}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{dx}{1-x}\>x^{-\Omega-1}\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}\,.

(250)

B.3
Results for the boundary cross section

We now provide results and derivations for boundary corrections to soft matrix elements. We first consider the case of global-soft function. We then present results for the boundary corrections to the
S_{c_{g}}
function, that would be needed to compute the boundary cross section in the small and intermediate
R_{g}
regions. Finally, we give an explicit result for the kernel
{\cal Q}^{\kappa}_{\varepsilon}
defined in Eq. (
119
).

B.3.1
Global-soft function with shifted soft drop

Here we derive the one-loop expression of the global-soft function with shifted soft drop condition, following the analogous derivation preformed for the collinear-soft function in Sec.
4.4
. In analogy with Eq. (
101
) we can write

\displaystyle S_{G}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\Theta_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon),\mu\bigr)=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{(\mu^{2}e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int\frac{dp^{+}dp^{-}}{(p^{+}p^{-})^{1+\epsilon}}\Theta_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)\overline{\Theta}_{R}

\displaystyle\qquad=S_{G}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\mu\bigr)+\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\mu\bigr)+{\cal O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,

(251)

where the first term is the global soft function for the cross section cumulant in
R_{g}
that we computed in Eq. (
217
), the constraint
\overline{\Theta}_{R}
was given in Eq. (
219
), while
\Theta_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)
refers to the soft drop failing condition with
\varepsilon
shift (without small angle approximation):

\displaystyle\Theta_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\equiv\Theta\bigg(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\Big(\sin\frac{\theta_{p}}{2}\Big)^{\beta}-\frac{p^{+}+p^{-}}{Q}-\varepsilon\bigg)

(252)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\rm sd}-\varepsilon\delta\bigg(\frac{p^{+}+p^{-}}{Q}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\Big(\sin\frac{\theta_{p}}{2}\Big)^{\beta}\bigg)+{\cal O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,.

The
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
correction in Eq. (
B.3.1
) is then given by substituting the soft drop condition with
-\delta_{\rm sd}
term in Eq. (
252
), which fixes
p^{-}
to the following value:

\displaystyle p^{-}=Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{2}{2+\beta}}(p^{+})^{\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}\bigg[1-\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{2+\beta}}\bigg]\,,\qquad p^{+}<Q_{\rm cut}\,.

(253)

This leads to

\displaystyle S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\mu\bigr)=-\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{2\epsilon}\frac{(e^{\gamma_{E}})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}

(254)

\displaystyle\times\int\frac{dp^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{-\frac{2(1+\beta)}{2+\beta}(1+\epsilon)}\bigg[1-\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{2+\beta}}\bigg]^{-(1+\epsilon)}\Theta\bigg(\sin\frac{R}{2}-\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}\bigg)\,.

We first consider the case of
\beta=0
for which the result is given by

\displaystyle S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta=0,\mu\bigr)+\text{virtual term}=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\rm UV}}+2\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan\frac{R}{2}}\Big)\bigg]\,.

(255)

Note that we only considered the real emission term in the measurement function in Eq. (
B.3.1
). We then demand that the virtual term, which we have not explicitly included, appropriately turns the IR pole in Eq. (
255
) into a UV pole.

Next we consider the case of
\beta>0
. Here, we note that the integral is power law IR divergent and will be set to zero in dimensional regularization. This results from the fact that we are trying to factorize the soft function at subleading power but without considering the full set of operators that appear at this order.
Hence, in order to resolve this ambiguity it is helpful to consider the combined measurement resulting from the shifted soft drop condition in Eq. (
252
), the
R_{g}
constraint in Eq. (
99
) (without small-angle approximation), the jet radius constraint in Eq. (
219
), along with
p^{+}
measurement applied to the matrix element defined by a single soft mode (i.e. not factorizing it into a global-soft and a collinear-soft mode). In this case, the measurement function is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm full}\big(p^{+},\ell^{+},R_{g}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\overline{\Theta}_{R}\bigg[\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)\overline{\Theta}_{R_{g}}\delta(\ell^{+}-p^{+})+\Theta_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)\delta(\ell^{+})\bigg]+\Theta_{R}\delta(\ell^{+})-\delta(\ell^{+})

\displaystyle=\overline{\Theta}_{R}\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)\big[\overline{\Theta}_{R_{g}}\delta(\ell^{+}-p^{+})-\delta(\ell^{+})\big]\,,

(256)

where the three terms in the first line correspond to emissions that pass the jet radius constraint, that fail and the virtual term. The ones that are clustered in the jet radius are then tested for (shifted) soft drop condition and the
R_{g}
constraint as in Eq. (
98
).
Writing
\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon)=\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}+\varepsilon\delta_{\rm sd}
and using the
\delta
function to fix
p^{-}
to Eq. (
253
), we find

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dp^{-}\>\overline{\Theta}_{R}\big[\overline{\Theta}_{R_{g}}\delta(\ell^{+}-p^{+})-\delta(\ell^{+})\big]\delta\bigg(p^{-}-Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{2}{2+\beta}}(p^{+})^{\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}\bigg[1-\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{2+\beta}}\bigg]\bigg)

(257)

\displaystyle=\Theta\bigg(\sin\frac{R_{g}}{2}-\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}\bigg)\big[\delta(\ell^{+}-p^{+})-\delta(\ell^{+})\big]+\Theta\bigg(\sin\frac{R_{g}}{2}<\Big(\frac{p^{+}}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2+\beta}}<\sin\frac{R}{2}\bigg)\delta(\ell^{+})\,.

The first term in the second line when expanded in small-angle approximation matches with the corresponding term in the measurement function that we used above for the
\ell^{+}
-differential collinear-soft function
S_{c}
in Eq. (
98
). The second term proportional to
\delta(\ell^{+})
is of interest to us and when integrated over
p^{+}
leads precisely to the bare
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
correction in Eq. (
254
) but now with
p^{+}
integral bounded below due to
R_{g}
measurement, which then renders it IR finite. This
R_{g}
-dependent term in the factorized cross section is captured in the
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
modification to the collinear-soft functions: the
\ell^{+}
-differential
S_{c}
, as seen in Eq. (
102
), or equivalently in the cumulative-
R_{g}

S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}
function if we are considering intermediate
R_{g}
regime as we show below.
Thus, we can consider the integration in Eq. (
254
) including the lower bound on
p^{+}
due to
R_{g}
measurement, and identify the pieces depending on
R
and
R_{g}
as parts of the global-soft and the collinear-soft functions respectively.
We then arrive at the following result for
\beta>0
:

\displaystyle S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}

\displaystyle\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta,\mu\bigr)=

(258)

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\beta}{2}\not\in\mathbb{N}\Big)\qquad

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}B\Big[\sin^{2}\Big(\frac{R}{2}\Big),-\frac{\beta}{2},0\Big]

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\beta}{2}\in\mathbb{N}\Big)\qquad

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\Bigg[\log\Big[\tan^{2}\Big(\frac{R}{2}\Big)\Big]+\sum_{k=1}^{\beta/2}\frac{1}{k}\Big[\sin^{2}\Big(\frac{R}{2}\Big)\Big]^{-k}\Bigg]\,.

The result for
S_{c_{g},\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1]\rm bare}
is simply negative of the result for
S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}
with
R
replaced by
R_{g}
and dropping the subleading terms in
R_{g}\ll R
. This leads to the result presented below in Eq. (
263
).

The terms in Eq. (
258
) are included in the
\Delta S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa}
piece in Eq. (
4.4.2
) for NLL
′
resummation as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta S_{G,\varepsilon}^{\kappa}\bigl(Q_{\rm cut},R,\beta\bigr)=\Theta(\beta>0)\Bigg[1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{gs})\beta_{0}}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan^{1+\beta}\frac{R}{2}}\Big)\Bigg]

(259)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{gs})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\Bigg[\frac{2}{\beta}-\sin^{\beta}\frac{R}{2}\bigg(\Theta(0<\beta<2)\>B\Big[\sin^{2}\frac{R}{2},1-\frac{\beta}{2},0\Big]+\delta_{\beta,2}\log\tan^{2}\frac{R}{2}\bigg)\Bigg]\,,

where as in the case of
R_{g}
-dependent collinear-soft function in Eq. (
79
) we have included a
\beta_{0}
term to cancel the
\mu
dependence due to the running coupling.

B.3.2
Boundary corrections to the collinear-soft kernel

Here we explicitly solve the Laplace transforms in Eq. (
119
), which define the evolution kernel
{\cal Q}^{\kappa}_{\varepsilon}
governing boundary corrections to the cross section in the large
R_{g}
region.
The
\Delta S_{c,\varepsilon}^{[C_{\kappa}]}
piece defined in Eq. (
112
) transforms into

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{S}_{c,\varepsilon}^{[C_{\kappa}]}\Bigl(z=s\,Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\Big)^{2+\beta},\beta,\mu\Bigr)

(260)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}z^{\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}\bigg[\Theta(\beta>0)\>\Gamma\Big(\!-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}\Big)-\Gamma\bigg(\!-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta},z\bigg)\bigg]\,,

where the incomplete gamma function was defined in Eq. (
240
).
Using this result we find

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho}\varrho^{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{S}_{c,\varepsilon}^{[C_{\kappa}]}(\varrho\upsilon,\beta,\mu_{cs})=\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\upsilon^{\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}\Bigg(\Theta(\beta>0)\>B\Big[-\Omega,-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}\Big]-\Theta(1-\upsilon)B\Big[1-\upsilon;\,-\Omega,-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}\Big]\Bigg)\,.

(261)

We now state the result for the second term in Eq. (
119
), which involves again Eq. (
239
)

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho}\varrho^{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{S}_{c,\varepsilon}^{[C_{\kappa}]}(\varrho\upsilon,\beta,\mu_{cs})\Delta\tilde{S}_{c}^{[C_{\kappa}]}(\varrho\upsilon,\beta,\mu_{cs})=-\Bigg(\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\Bigg)^{2}\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}

(262)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\upsilon^{-b}\Bigg(\Theta(\beta>0)B[-\Omega,b]\Theta(1-\upsilon)\int_{0}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{dx}{1-x}\>x^{b-\Omega-1}\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\Theta\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\upsilon\Big)\int_{\upsilon}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{dx}{1-x}\>x^{b-\Omega-1}\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}\>B\Big[1-\frac{\upsilon}{x},-\Omega,b\Big]\Bigg)\Bigg|_{b=\frac{-\beta}{2+\beta}}\,.

B.3.3
Boundary Corrections for intermediate and small
R_{g}

Here we discuss the soft drop boundary cross section in the intermediate and small
R_{g}
regimes.
In both of the regimes where
R_{g}\ll\theta_{g}^{\star}
it is the
S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}
function that receives
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
corrections upon shift to the soft drop condition. This allows to treat the two cases where on equal footing and results in considerable simplifications, since none of the boundary corrections now depend on the jet mass.
In this work we only provide the details of the boundary corrections in the soft sector in these regimes and leave further studies of the corresponding resummation to future work.

As commented below Eq. (
258
), the results for boundary corrections to
S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}
are closely related to the corresponding corrections to the global-soft function. Following the reasoning described there, we find

\displaystyle S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}

\displaystyle\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon),\beta,\mu\Bigr]-S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa[1],\rm bare}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Bigr]

(263)

\displaystyle(\beta=0)\qquad=-\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+2\log\Big(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}(\frac{R_{g}}{2})}\Big)\bigg]\,,

\displaystyle(\beta>0)\qquad=-\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\Big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\Big)^{-\beta}\frac{2}{\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\,.

Again, as required by consistency with RGE and the large
R_{g}
case, for
\beta=0
we find a UV pole, which cancels against the global-soft function in Eq. (
4.4.1
). Hence, we have an additional running between the scales
\mu_{gs}
and
\mu_{cs_{g}}
resulting from the non-cusp anomalous dimension

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})=-\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})=-8C_{\kappa}\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\,.\qquad(\beta=0)

(264)

In analogy with the treatment of the boundary corrections to the global-soft function in Eq. (
4.4.2
), we write the
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
corrections to the CS
g
function as

\displaystyle S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon),\beta,\mu\Bigr]

\displaystyle=S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Bigr]-2\delta_{\beta,0}\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}L_{cs_{g}}

(265)

\displaystyle+\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\frac{\Theta(\beta>0)}{\big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\big)^{\beta}}S_{c_{g}}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta,\mu\Bigr]\Delta S_{c_{g},\varepsilon}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta\Bigr]\,,

where

\displaystyle\Delta S_{c_{g},\varepsilon}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Theta(\beta>0)\frac{-2}{\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\bigg(1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)\beta_{0}}{2\pi}L_{cs_{g}}\bigg)

(266)

and the logarithm being

\displaystyle L_{cs_{g}}\equiv\log\Bigg(\frac{\mu}{Q_{\rm cut}\big(\frac{R_{g}}{2}\big)^{1+\beta}}\Bigg)\,.

(267)

Like in Eq. (
79
) for the large-
R_{g}
collinear-soft function, Eq. (
265
) includes an additional
\beta_{0}
term to compensate for the
\mu
dependence of the running coupling, such that the non-logarithmic correction
\Delta S_{c_{g},\varepsilon}^{\kappa}\Bigl[\frac{R_{g}}{2}Q_{\rm cut}^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},\beta\Bigr]
is explicitly
\mu
independent at NLL
′
. This result consistently matches with the boundary correction in the large
R_{g}
region stated above in Eq. (
B.3.2
). To see this we can take
\upsilon\rightarrow 0
limit in Eq. (
B.3.2
) finding

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\upsilon\rightarrow 0}\int\frac{d\varrho}{2\pi i}\>e^{\varrho}\varrho^{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{S}_{c,\varepsilon}^{[C_{\kappa}]}(\varrho\upsilon,\beta,\mu_{cs})

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}\,,

(268)

which results in the right coefficient so as to match onto the boundary correction in Eq. (
266
).

B.4
Calculation of
C_{2}^{q}
from the differential boundary cross section

Here we set up the calculation of
C_{2}^{q}
based on the cross section differential in groomed jet radius, i.e. by taking the
R_{g}
derivative of the large
R_{g}
cumulative cross section with boundary corrections described in Sec.
4.4
. Although the extensions to
pp
collisions is straightforward, here we limit ourselves to the
e^{+}e^{-}
case. We first consider the case of
\beta>0
and then include the additional terms that appear for
\beta=0
at NLL. Based on the relation with the moment
M_{-1}^{q\varocircle}
defined in Eq. (
5
) adapted for the
e^{+}e^{-}
case considered here and following our prescription for computing the large
R_{g}
differential boundary cross section in Eq. (
193
),
C_{2}^{q}(m_{J}^{2})
is given by

\displaystyle C^{q}_{2}(m_{J}^{2})\frac{d\sigma}{dm_{J}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\int_{\theta_{\rm min}}^{\theta_{\rm max}}\!\!\!\!d\theta_{g}\,\frac{2}{\theta_{g}}w_{\rm large}(m_{J}^{2},\theta_{g})\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\>\frac{d\sigma(\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon))}{dm_{J}^{2}d\theta_{g}}\bigg|_{{\rm large}\,R_{g},\varepsilon\to 0}\,,

(269)

where the double differential boundary cross section is computed in the large
R_{g}
theory and is matched to zero at small angle using the weight function
w_{\rm large}
as described in Sec.
5
.
We switch to the auxiliary variable
\upsilon=(\theta_{g}/\theta_{g}^{\star})^{2+\beta}
introduced in Eq. (
119
),

\displaystyle C^{q}_{2}(m_{J}^{2})\frac{d\sigma}{dm_{J}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\int_{\upsilon_{\rm min}}^{\upsilon_{\rm max}}\!\!\!\!d\upsilon\,\frac{2}{\theta_{g}(\upsilon)}w_{\rm large}(m_{J}^{2},\theta_{g}(\upsilon))\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\>\frac{d\sigma(\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon))}{dm_{J}^{2}d\upsilon}\bigg|_{{\rm large}\,R_{g},\varepsilon\to 0}\,,

(270)

and following the decomposition of the cumulant in Eq. (
115
) we write

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma(\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon))}{dm_{J}^{2}d\upsilon}\equiv\frac{d}{d\upsilon}\frac{d\Sigma(R_{g}(\upsilon),\overline{\Theta}_{\rm sd}(\varepsilon))}{dm_{J}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\upsilon}\frac{d\Sigma\big(R_{g}(v),\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big)}{dm_{J}^{2}}+\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\frac{d\Delta\sigma_{\varepsilon}}{dm_{J}^{2}d\upsilon}\,,

(271)

where each of the two terms in the r.h.s. is defined as the
\upsilon
-derivative of the equivalent contribution in Eq. (
115
). The first term is only present for
\beta=0
, while the second contributes for each
\beta
.

We now compute the two terms in turn, starting from the second. This requires differentiating Eq. (
120
), where we note that the term involving
S^{q}_{G}
is independent of
\theta_{g}
(or
\upsilon
),

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta\sigma_{\varepsilon}}{dm_{J}^{2}d\upsilon}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{2}{\theta_{g}^{\star}}\Big)^{\beta}N^{q}_{\rm evol}\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\upsilon}\Big(v^{-\frac{\beta}{2+\beta}}{\cal Q}_{\varepsilon}^{q}[\Omega,\upsilon,\beta]\Big)

(272)

\displaystyle=v^{\frac{-\beta}{2+\beta}}\Big(\frac{2}{\theta_{g}^{\star}}\Big)^{\beta}N^{q}_{\rm evol}\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]\bigg[\frac{e^{\gamma_{E}\Omega}}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\Big)^{i}{\cal R}^{[i]}_{\varepsilon}\big(\Omega,\upsilon,\beta\big)\bigg]\,.

In the second step we extracted the operator
\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]
from the definition on
{\cal Q}_{\varepsilon}^{q}
in Eq. (
119
) (omitting for brevity the remaining arguments) and we organized the remaining
\upsilon
dependent terms as a perturbative expansion in
\alpha_{s}
.
For simplicity we suppress the arguments of the normalization factor
N^{q}_{\rm evol}
defined in Eq. (
61
).
By taking derivatives of the inverse Laplace transforms of respectively Eqs. (
B.3.2
) and (
262
), we determine the coefficients
{\cal R}^{[1]}_{\varepsilon}
and
{\cal R}^{[2]}_{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle{\cal R}^{[1]}_{\varepsilon}\big(\Omega,\upsilon,\beta\big)

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{0}^{-\Omega}(1-\upsilon)\,,

(273)

\displaystyle{\cal R}^{[2]}_{\varepsilon}\big(\Omega,\upsilon,\beta\big)

\displaystyle=B[-\Omega,b]\Theta(1-\upsilon)\upsilon^{-b}B[1-\upsilon,b-\Omega,0]-\Theta(\beta>0)\Theta\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\upsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt\times\int_{\upsilon}^{1-\upsilon}\!\!\!\frac{dx}{1-x}\>\frac{1}{x^{1+\Omega}}\Bigg(\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}\Big(1-\frac{\upsilon}{x}\Big)^{-(1+\Omega)}+\Big(\frac{\upsilon}{x}\Big)^{-b}B\Big[1-\frac{\upsilon}{x},-\Omega,b\Big]\Bigg)\Bigg|_{b=\frac{-\beta}{2+\beta}}\,.

(274)

Let us now move on to the first term in Eq. (
271
), which is the
\upsilon
-derivative of Eq. (
116
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\upsilon}\frac{d\Sigma\big(R_{g}(v),\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big)}{dm_{J}^{2}}=N^{q}_{\rm evol}\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega},\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\>\frac{d}{d\upsilon}{\cal Q}^{q}\big[\Omega,\upsilon,\beta\big]

(275)

\displaystyle\qquad=N^{q}_{\rm evol}\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega},\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\>\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]\frac{e^{\gamma_{E}\Omega}}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}\bigg(\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\bigg)\frac{1}{\upsilon}B\big[1-\upsilon,-\Omega,0\big]\,.

Here we used that
d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega},\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big]
does not depend on the groomed jet radius, extracted
S_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]
from the definition of
{\cal Q}^{q}
in Eq. (
249
), and took the
\upsilon
derivative of the inverse Laplace transform in Eq. (
239
).
Expanding the operator
d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega},\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big]
to
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega},\delta_{\beta,0}\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}=\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\bigg[1+\delta_{\beta,0}\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\eta\big(\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut}),\mu_{cs},\mu_{gs}\big)\bigg]

(276)

\displaystyle\qquad+\delta_{\beta,0}\frac{Q\varepsilon}{Q_{\rm cut}}\frac{d\sigma^{q}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\rightarrow 0\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\bigg[\frac{2\alpha_{s}(\mu_{gs})C_{F}}{\pi}\log\Big(\frac{\mu_{gs}}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan\frac{R}{2}}\Big)-\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\partial_{\Omega}\bigg]\,,

where the kernel
\eta\big(\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut})\big)
in Eq. (
204
) arises from the
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
expansion of the Sudakov exponent, and the additional terms in the second line result from the
{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
logarithms in the global-soft and collinear soft function in Eqs. (
4.4.2
) and (
110
) respectively. Note that after taking inverse Laplace transform the log in the collinear-soft function is replaced by the derivative
\partial_{\Omega}
. Additionally, the two terms are evaluated at different
\mu
arguments. Furthermore,
d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega}\rightarrow 0\big]
denotes setting to zero
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
(or higher) corrections in the term
d\sigma[\partial_{\Omega}]
so as to account purely for the evolution factor in Eq. (
4.3
).
We can now finally combine Eqs. (
272
) and (
275
) into the double differential boundary cross section in Eq. (
271
). Substituting in Eq. (
270
) yields our final formula

\displaystyle C^{q}_{2}(m_{J}^{2})\frac{d\sigma}{dm_{J}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{J}^{2}}{QQ_{\rm cut}}N^{q}_{\rm evol}\int_{\upsilon_{\rm min}}^{\upsilon_{\rm max}}\frac{d\upsilon}{\upsilon}\,\frac{2}{\theta_{g}(\upsilon)}w_{\rm large}(m_{J}^{2},\theta_{g}(\upsilon))

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Bigg(\delta_{\beta,0}\frac{d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega}\rightarrow 0\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\bigg[\frac{2\alpha_{s}(\mu_{gs})C_{F}}{\pi}\log\Big(\frac{\mu_{gs}}{Q_{\rm cut}\tan\frac{R}{2}}\Big)-\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\partial_{\Omega}\bigg]B\big[1-\upsilon,-\Omega,0\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\frac{d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]}{dm_{J}^{2}}\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]\bigg\{\delta_{\beta,0}\,\eta\big(\gamma_{0}(\varepsilon,z_{\rm cut}),\mu_{cs},\mu_{gs}\big)B\big[1-\upsilon,-\Omega,0\big]

\displaystyle+v^{\frac{1+\beta}{2+\beta}}\Big(\frac{2}{\theta_{g}^{\star}}\Big)^{\beta}\Big[{\cal R}_{\varepsilon}^{[1]}(\Omega,\upsilon,\beta)+\Big(\frac{2}{2+\beta}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{cs})C_{F}}{\pi}\Big){\cal R}_{\varepsilon}^{[2]}(\Omega,\upsilon,\beta)\Big]\bigg\}\Bigg)\frac{e^{\gamma_{E}\Omega}}{\Gamma(-\Omega)}\,.

(277)

As discussed in the introduction, the geometry relevant to the non-perturbative corrections multiplying
C_{2}^{q}
requires computing this Wilson coefficient using only the large
R_{g}
theory, which is enforced by the weight function
w_{\rm large}(m_{J}^{2},\theta(\upsilon))
in Eq. (
B.4
). This is consistent with the idea that
C_{2}^{q}
should describe only perturbative physics. We note however that the integrand has an overall behavior
\sim 1/\upsilon
, which enhances NP effects at low jet mass. To make sure that there are no residual NP effects in
C_{2}^{q}
, we adopt a prescription analogous to the one used for freezing the scale
\mu_{c_{g}}
in Eq. (
173
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\upsilon}\simeq\Big(\frac{\psi^{\star}}{\psi}\Big)^{2+\beta}\;\to\;\frac{(\psi^{\star})^{2+\beta}}{\big[f_{\rm run}(\psi^{1+\beta})\big]^{\frac{2+\beta}{1+\beta}}}\,,

(278)

where
\psi,\psi^{\star}
are the auxiliary variables defined in Eqs. (
160
) and (
166
), and the first equality is exact in the
R_{g}\ll 1
limit. The profile function
f_{\rm run}
defined in Eq. (
5.3.2
) smoothly freezes its argument when approaching non-perturbative values.

As a final remark, we notice that in the region
m_{J}^{2}<m_{0}^{2}
where grooming is active,
\upsilon_{\rm max}=1
and Eq. (
273
) requires solving the class of integrals

\displaystyle I_{n}(\upsilon_{\rm min},\Omega)=\partial_{\Omega}^{n}\big[I_{0}(\upsilon_{\rm min},\Omega)\big]\equiv\int_{\upsilon_{\rm min}}^{1}\textrm{d}\upsilon\frac{\big(-\textrm{ln}(1-\upsilon)\big)^{n}}{(1-\upsilon)^{\Omega+1}}f_{n}(\upsilon)\,,

(279)

where the derivatives arise from the operators
d\sigma\big[\partial_{\Omega}\big]
and
\tilde{S}_{c}^{[\beta_{0}]}[\partial_{\Omega}\big]
in Eq. (
B.4
).
To avoid numerical instabilities, we deal with these integrals using the subtraction

\displaystyle I_{n}(\upsilon_{\rm min},\Omega)=\int_{\upsilon_{\rm min}}^{1}\textrm{d}\upsilon\frac{\big(-\textrm{ln}(1-\upsilon)\big)^{i}}{(1-\upsilon)^{\Omega+1}}\big[f_{i}(\upsilon)-f_{i}(1)\big]+f_{i}(1)\bigg(\int_{\upsilon_{\rm min}}^{1}\textrm{d}\upsilon\frac{\big(-\textrm{ln}(1-\upsilon)\big)^{i}}{(1-\upsilon)^{\Omega+1}}\bigg)\,.

(280)

Similarly, we handle the singular integrand in the first term in Eq. (
274
) using

\displaystyle{\cal I}(v,\Omega)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\upsilon}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{dx}{1-x}\>\frac{1}{x^{1+\Omega}}\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}\Big(1-\frac{\upsilon}{x}\Big)^{-(1+\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\upsilon}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{dx}{1-x}\>\frac{1}{(x-v)^{1+\Omega}}\log\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{\upsilon}^{1-\upsilon}\frac{f(x)-f(\upsilon)}{(x-\upsilon)^{1+\Omega}}-f(\upsilon)\frac{(1-2\upsilon)^{-\Omega}}{(-\Omega)}\,,

(281)

where

\displaystyle f(x)\equiv\frac{1}{1-x}\log\Big(\frac{1-x}{\upsilon}\Big)\,.

(282)

Appendix C
Calculation of NGLs and Clustering effects

C.1
Nonglobal logarithms

The functions
\Xi_{S}^{q},\Xi_{C}^{q}
introduced in Eqs. (
42
) and (
52
) can be expanded as a perturbative series in
\alpha_{s}
. Here we compute the first nontrivial correction, which arise at
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
, starting from the function
\Xi_{S}^{q}
that describes the NGLs in the intermediate
R_{g}
region. To avoid plus distributions we switch to the function cumulative in jet mass. Following
Kang:2019prh
, when the clustering effects are ignored we can write

\displaystyle\Xi_{S}^{q}(t_{S})=1-C_{F}C_{A}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\int_{1\in J}dc_{1}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{2\pi}\int_{2\not\in J}dc_{2}\frac{d\phi_{2}}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Theta(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta})\,\Theta\left(\frac{\theta_{c}^{2}}{4}-\frac{x_{1}}{2}(1-c_{1})\right)\frac{\cos\phi_{2}}{(1-c_{1}c_{2}-s_{1}s_{2}\cos\phi_{2})s_{1}s_{2}}

(283)

where
c_{i}=\cos\theta_{i}
,
s_{i}=\sin\theta_{i}
and the dependence on the (cumulative) jet mass variable is implicit in
(\theta_{c}/2)^{2}=(\theta_{g}^{\star}/2)^{2+\beta}Q_{\rm cut}/Q
.
Here the integration variables
x_{i},\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{i}
represent respectively the energy fractions, colatitudes, and azimuths of the strongly ordered emissions. The geometry relevant to the NGLs requires the largest emission to lie inside the groomed jet and the second emission to lie out of it.

The energy integrals can be done easily and yield

\displaystyle\Xi_{S}^{q}(t_{S})

\displaystyle=1-C_{F}C_{A}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int_{1\in J}dc_{1}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{2\pi}\int_{2\not{\in}J}dc_{2}\frac{d\phi_{2}}{2\pi}\Theta\left(\frac{\theta_{c}^{2}}{2\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta}}-(1-c_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\cos\phi_{2}}{(1-c_{1}c_{2}-s_{1}s_{2}\cos\phi_{2})s_{1}s_{2}}\frac{1}{2}\textrm{ln}^{2}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{c}^{2}}{2(1-c_{1})\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta}}\bigg)\,.

(284)

Since the gluon 1 is restricted to be inside the jet,
\theta_{1}/2\leq R_{g}/2\ll 1
, we can approximate the factor of
1-c_{1}
inside the logarithm as
\theta_{1}^{2}/2
. At the same time, taking into account clustering effects as in
Kang:2019prh
, the dominant term in the
R_{g}/2\ll 1
limit gives the following result at two loops:

\displaystyle\Xi_{S}^{q}(t_{S})\,=\,1-\frac{4}{9}C_{F}C_{A}\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}t_{S}^{2}\,.

(285)

The leading NGLs in the other two regions can be obtained from the intermediate
R_{g}
expression in Eq. (
45
) by taking appropriate limits. For the large groomed jet radius regime
R_{g}=\theta_{g}^{\star}
and the NGL contribution goes to 0 as was expected. For the small groomed jet radius regime we can formally set
\theta_{c}=R_{g}
and the dominant contribution in this EFT is

\displaystyle\Xi_{C}^{q}(t_{C})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{4}{9}C_{F}C_{A}\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}t_{C}^{2}\,,

(286)

which is larger than the contribution for the case of intermediate groomed jet radius. Written with
t_{C}
see that Eq. (
286
) has the same form as Eq. (
285
).

C.2
Abelian clustering logarithms

In this section we account for the leading logarithms arising from clustering effects of the C/A algorithm while implementing soft drop for independent emissions.
To this aim, we first consider a naive implementation of the soft drop algorithm where clustering logarithms are absent and see how clustering effects modify this result. The naive implementation simply tests for the SD condition for each particle independently, i.e. the allowed phase space for each emission is

\displaystyle\Theta(z_{i}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{i}^{\beta})\Theta(R_{g}-\theta_{i})+\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{i}^{\beta}-z_{i})\,.

(287)

However, we know that the C/A algorithm recursively clusters partons with minimum angular separation; since the SD algorithm works backwards through the history of the clustered jet, the grooming condition is then effectively applied on subjets instead of individual partons. Therefore, (in the case of 2 collinear-soft emissions that gives the leading configuration) we can have a scenario where one of the partons would fail soft drop and the other would pass if tested independently, but the two get clustered with each other first and with the collinear parton later. The SD condition is now tested on the entire collinear-soft subjet, which will pass the test. In this case the softest parton has been pulled inside the groomed jet, violating the naive measure in Eq. (
287
). Instead, in case the most energetic collinear-soft emission is clustered with the collinear parton first, then the naive implementation of the measurement is correct.

Let us denote the portion of phase space where the naive measure is violated by

\displaystyle\Theta_{C/A}=\Theta(d_{1}-R_{g}^{2})\Theta(R_{g}^{2}-d_{2})\Theta(d_{2}-d_{12})\,,

(288)

where
d_{12}
is the relative distance of the collinear soft emissions and
d_{i}
their distance from the jet axis, so that parton 1 is at a distance greater then
R_{g}
from the jet axis while parton 2 is within
R_{g}
from it. For 1 and 2 to be clustered first, we then need
d_{2}>d_{12}
. Expressed in the small angle approximation, this becomes

\displaystyle\Theta_{C/A}=\Theta(\theta^{2}_{1}-R_{g}^{2})\Theta(R_{g}^{2}-\theta_{2}^{2})\Theta(-\theta_{1}^{2}+2\theta_{1}\theta_{2}\cos\phi_{2})\,,

(289)

where we have assumed without loss of generality that
\phi_{12}=\phi_{2}
.
The allowed phase space requires that parton 1 must fail SD while parton 2 can either pass or fail (with energy fractions
x_{2}\gg x_{1}
). However, for parton 1 to be pulled inside the jet, we need that parton 2 passes soft drop. If we ignore for the time being the mass measurement, and consider only a cumulative
R_{g}
measurement, the naive result without clustering effects reads

\displaystyle M_{\rm indep.}(R_{g})

\displaystyle\equiv\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{1}^{\beta}-x_{1})\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta_{C/A}\,,

(290)

where we have abbreviated the two-parton phase space in the strongly ordered limit as

\displaystyle\int d\Pi_{2}=\left(\alpha_{s}\frac{C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\int\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\int\frac{d\theta_{1}}{\theta_{1}}\int\frac{d\theta_{2}}{\theta_{2}}\int\frac{d\phi_{1}}{2\pi}\int\frac{d\phi_{2}}{2\pi}\,.

(291)

Let us now account for clustering effects. The idea is that since parton 1 is pulled inside the jet, it never gets individually tested for SD and can in fact also be allowed to pass SD in this region of phase space. Therefore the measurement in Eq. (
290
) gets modified to give us

\displaystyle M_{\rm cluster}(R_{g})

\displaystyle\equiv\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta_{C/A}\,.

(292)

We now express the result as a correction on top of the naive result by subtracting it out the from the full result to isolate the clustering effects:

\displaystyle\Delta M_{\rm alg}(R_{g})

\displaystyle\equiv M_{\rm cluster}-M_{\rm indep.}=\frac{1}{2!}\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Theta(x_{1}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta_{C/A}\,,

(293)

which agrees with the integrand quoted in
Kang:2019prh
. The factor of
1/2!
takes into account the scenario where we interchange the labels 1 and 2 in the strongly ordered phase space measure in Eq. (
291
).

We can now extend this result to our case where we also have a jet mass measurement on the groomed jet. The naive value in Eq. (
290
) with a cumulative jet mass measurement becomes

\displaystyle M_{\rm indep.}(m_{J}^{2},R_{g})

\displaystyle\equiv\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{1}^{\beta}-x_{1})\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta\Big((\theta_{c}(m_{J}^{2}))^{2}-x_{2}\theta_{2}^{2}\Big)\Theta_{C/A}\,,

(294)

which upon including clustering corrections gives

\displaystyle M_{\rm cluster}(m_{J}^{2},R_{g})

\displaystyle\equiv\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{1}\theta_{1}^{2})\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{2}\theta_{2}^{2})\Theta_{C/A}\,,

(295)

where we note that the parton 1 will contribute to the jet mass regardless of whether it passes or fails since it is part of our groomed jet. Therefore the correction from the algorithm becomes

\displaystyle\Delta M_{\rm alg}(m_{J}^{2},R_{g})\equiv\int d\Pi_{2}\,\Big[\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{1}\theta_{1}^{2})-\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{1}^{\beta}-x_{1})\Big]\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{2}\theta_{2}^{2})\Theta_{C/A}\,.

(296)

To extract out the leading logarithmic behavior, we notice that none of the terms in Eq. (
296
) are sensitive to the difference between
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, and hence can approximate
\theta_{1}\sim\theta_{2}\sim R_{g}
in the integrand, such that

\displaystyle\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{1}\theta_{1}^{2})-\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{1}^{\beta}-x_{1})

\displaystyle\approx\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{1}R_{g}^{2})-\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta}-x_{1})\,,

(297)

\displaystyle\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}\theta_{2}^{\beta})\,\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{2}\theta_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\approx\Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta})\,\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{2}R_{g}^{2})\,,

which allow us to factorize the angular and energy integrals,

\displaystyle\Delta M_{\rm alg}(m_{J}^{2},R_{g})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\int\frac{d\theta_{1}}{\theta_{1}}\int\frac{d\theta_{2}}{\theta_{2}}\int\frac{d\phi_{1}}{2\pi}\frac{d\phi_{2}}{2\pi}\Theta_{C/A}

(298)

\displaystyle\times\int\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\Big[\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{1}R_{g}^{2})-\Theta(\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta}-x_{1})\Big]\int\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\ \Theta(x_{2}-\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta})\Theta(\theta_{c}^{2}-x_{2}R_{g}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\textrm{ln}^{2}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{c}^{2}}{\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta+2}}\bigg)\int\frac{d\theta_{1}}{\theta_{1}}\int\frac{d\theta_{2}}{\theta_{2}}\int\frac{d\phi_{1}}{2\pi}\int\frac{d\phi_{2}}{2\pi}\Theta_{C/A}\,.

The angular integral is now identical to the one in
Kang:2019prh
, which can be solved to yield

\displaystyle\Delta M_{\rm alg}(m_{J}^{2},R_{g})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{\kappa}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}}{54}\textrm{ln}^{2}\left(\frac{\big(\theta_{c}(m_{J}^{2})\big)^{2}}{\tilde{z}_{\rm cut}R_{g}^{\beta+2}}\right)\,.

(299)

We see that the leading abelian clustering logarithms have the same functional form as the NGLs in Eq. (
45
), but are further suppressed by the factor
\frac{1}{36}
.
Furthermore, this result shows that just like the NGLs, the leading clustering logarithms also vanish in the large
R_{g}
regime, consistent with the existence of a single collinear-soft mode rather than two modes that know about
R_{g}
separated in virtuality. On the other hand, they are maximized in the small
R_{g}
limit,
\theta_{c}=R_{g}
, where the effect of the mass measurement drops out and we find back the result of
Kang:2019prh
.

We also note that in this calculation we have not considered the scenario when both partons naively fail the SD condition but pass if clustered together. This is evaluated in
Frye:2016aiz
and would lead to a sub-leading single logarithmic contribution
\alpha_{s}^{2}\log X
compared to the correction explicitly considered here.
